From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, doctor. Thank you for seeing me. Um, well, I guess what really made me finally come in today—and honestly, I've been putting this off for a really long time because it's just so embarrassing to talk about—but it's about what happens when I get home. \n\nI'm 19, and I'm juggling some college classes right now while also working as a financial manager, which, as you can imagine, is just incredibly stressful. I'm looking at spreadsheets and managing accounts all day, and my brain is just absolutely fried by the end of it. So, what happens is, after work or after my classes, I'll finally get home, and I'll just sit down with my laptop to try and unwind. I'll put on a show or a movie, just trying to turn my brain off for a minute. \n\nBut then... I just end up eating. And I don't mean just having a normal dinner. I'll get a whole takeout meal, and I'll eat all of it, but then I'll immediately move on to a huge bag of chips, and then I feel like I need sweets, so I'll eat whatever candy or cookies I have around, and then I'm literally just rummaging through my desk drawer for whatever random granola bars or snacks I've stashed in there from weeks ago. I just keep going and going. It's way, way more than I ever planned to eat when I first sat down with my food. And it's definitely way more than I would ever eat if I were around anyone else—like, I would never, ever do this in front of my friends or my coworkers. It's just me, alone in my room, and the sheer amount of food I'm going through every time I sit down at my laptop is just... it's really starting to scare me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Um... I'm not really sure if that's the right word for it, to be honest. I mean, I haven't really thought about it in terms of guilt. It’s more like... well, when it’s all over, and I’m just sitting there in the quiet, I’m usually just staring at my desk. And my desk is just covered, you know? Like, my laptop is pushed back, and there are all these empty wrappers—the takeout containers, the chip bags, the little plastic wrappers from the candies—just piled up right next to my finance textbooks and my work notes. \n\nAnd the show I was watching is usually over, the credits are rolling, and I just sit there feeling... I don't know, kind of blank? Like, I haven't really noticed feeling guilty, per se. I know people talk about 'food guilt' all the time, like when my coworkers say they feel guilty for eating a donut in the breakroom, but this isn't like that. I'm just sort of in a daze. I mostly just feel really, really physically full and kind of confused about how I just ate all of that without even realizing it. \n\nSo instead of sitting there thinking about guilt, I usually just immediately go into clean-up mode. I'll sweep all the trash into a plastic bag, tie it up super tight, and take it straight out to the big dumpster behind my apartment building so I don't have to look at it anymore. And then I just wash my face and try to go to sleep, because my alarm goes off at 6 AM for work and my brain is just exhausted. So, yeah, I don't know if I'd say I feel guilty... I'm just not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can stop eating once you've started on these occasions?\n\nPatient: Stop? Oh, wow. Um... no. No, I really don't. And that's actually the part that really, really freaks me out, because I'm usually such a controlled person, you know? Like, at work, I manage thousands of dollars, I balance accounts down to the penny, I have my whole schedule mapped out. But when I'm sitting there in my room with the food... it's like a switch flips. \n\nOnce I start, it honestly feels like I'm completely disconnected from my own body. Like, I’m watching myself from the outside, just watching my hand keep reaching for the next thing, and the next thing, and the next thing. And the craziest part is that my brain is still somewhat functioning in the background. Like, I'll literally be thinking to myself, *Jaime, stop, you have a budget report to finish for work tomorrow, you need to stop this right now.* And I'll even realize that I'm not even enjoying the food anymore! Like, the chips don't even taste good, they're just salty and scratching my throat, and the candy is just making my teeth ache, but my hand just keeps moving. It's like I'm on autopilot, or like a robot that's just been programmed to keep eating until everything in front of me is completely gone. I just can't make myself stop, no matter how much my brain is screaming at me to just put the wrapper down and go back to my spreadsheets. It's so bizarre and honestly just really terrifying to feel so out of control of my own hands.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of how much you eat during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Control? Um, no, definitely not. It's actually kind of what I was just trying to explain, because it's so hard to put into words, but it's like... it's like the total opposite of control. I mean, at work, I am the definition of control. I have to be. I'm managing these massive accounts, making sure every single decimal point is in the right place, tracking every expense. If I lose control there, people lose money, you know? So I'm used to being so disciplined. But then I get home, and I sit down with that food, and it's just... poof. All that discipline is just gone.\n\nIt's really the most bizarre feeling. It's exactly like I was saying—once I start eating, it feels like I'm completely detached from myself. Like I'm hovering somewhere above my desk, just watching this girl named Jaime keep reaching for the next thing. And the really scary part is that my rational brain is still turned on. I'm literally sitting there, watching my hand grab another handful of chips, and my inner monologue is going, *Jaime, stop, what are you doing? You have that huge budget report to finish for the quarterly review tomorrow morning, you need to focus, you need to stop this right now.* \n\nBut my hand just ignores my brain. It just keeps going. And I'm not even enjoying it! That's the part that makes me feel so crazy. I'll be chewing something, and I'll realize it doesn't even taste good anymore. My jaw will literally be tired from chewing, my stomach will feel stretched and uncomfortable, and I'll be thinking, *I don't even want this cookie, it's stale, why am I eating this?* But I just keep eating it anyway. It's like my body has been hijacked and I'm just a passenger watching it happen. So, no, I don't feel like I have any control at all. It feels like the food is completely controlling me until there's absolutely nothing left to eat.\n\nDoctor: How often do these eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: How often? Oh, wow, um... well, it's actually kind of a lot, to be honest. I mean, I didn't really realize how often it was happening at first because, like I said, I just kind of go into a daze and then throw all the evidence away in the big dumpster out back. But then, a couple of weeks ago, I was trying to reconcile my own personal budget—because, you know, I do that for work all day, so I'm usually really meticulous about my own finances, tracking every expense in this spreadsheet I built. And I started noticing all these charges.\n\nLike, I was looking through my bank statements and my food delivery apps, and there were just so many of them. Charges for two large pizzas here, a massive order of Chinese takeout there, plus all these convenience store runs for chips and candy that I didn't even really remember making. And I keep this journal, too, mostly just to track my work tasks and stress levels, but I started looking back through it, cross-referencing the dates with the delivery charges. \n\nAnd, um, it turns out it's been happening most weeks. Like, almost every single week since they announced the big restructuring at my firm. They laid off a bunch of people, and suddenly I was managing accounts that used to be handled by three different managers, and the pressure just skyrocketed. And looking at the dates, that's exactly when this all really kicked into high gear. It's not just a once-in-a-while thing, either, like, \"Oh, I had a bad day and ate too much.\" It's usually happening more than once a week. Sometimes it's Tuesday and Thursday, sometimes it's Wednesday, Friday, and Sunday. It's definitely not just here and there. It's becoming this regular, horrible routine that I can't seem to break, no matter how much I try to budget my money or schedule my time. It's just... it's happening a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel stressed, anxious, or upset right before these eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Stressed? Oh, um... well, it's hard to say exactly. I mean, my whole life is basically one giant ball of stress right now, so it's kind of difficult to pinpoint if I'm *specifically* feeling that way right before I start eating, you know? Like, I was just saying about the restructuring at the firm—ever since that happened, I feel like I've been operating at a ten out of ten on the anxiety scale basically from the moment I wake up until I finally manage to pass out at night. \n\nI'm constantly worried about messing up one of these new accounts, or missing a deadline, or that my boss is going to realize I'm in way over my head and fire me. So, by the time I get home, I'm already so wound up. But right before I actually start eating... I don't know if I'd say I feel a sudden spike in being upset or anxious right in that exact moment. It's more like a constant, low-level hum of panic that's just always there in the background now. \n\nSometimes I'll be sitting at my desk at home, trying to get ahead on some spreadsheets for the next day, and I just feel so completely drained and overwhelmed by it all. And then the next thing I know, I'm ordering the food. So, I guess I'm always stressed, but I haven't really noticed if there's a specific trigger or a sudden wave of emotion right before the eating starts. It all just kind of blends together into this one big, exhausting blur of pressure and then... eating. Does that make sense? I just haven't really paid attention to that exact moment before it happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop yourself from continuing to eat once you've started?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean, that's exactly it. It's like I was saying earlier, it's this total inability to just... stop. It's the craziest thing because, like I mentioned, I'm usually so disciplined. I mean, you don't get to be a financial manager at nineteen without having a ridiculous amount of self-control and focus, right? I skipped so many parties in college just to study and get ahead, so I know I have willpower. But with this... it's like my willpower just evaporates the second I take that first bite. \n\nOnce I start, it honestly feels like I'm completely disconnected from my own body. Like I'm just hovering there, watching myself keep reaching for the next thing, and the next thing. And my brain is fully active! I'm sitting there, literally thinking, *Jaime, stop, what are you doing? You have that huge budget report to finish for the partners tomorrow, you need to stop right now.* But my hand just keeps moving. I'll be eating something, and I'll realize I'm not even enjoying it anymore. Like, my stomach will be hurting, the food doesn't even taste good, and I'll be thinking, *Why am I still eating this stale cookie?* But I just can't make myself stop. It's like somebody else is driving the car, and I'm just trapped in the passenger seat watching it all happen until there's literally nothing left in front of me. So yeah, unable to stop is exactly how I'd describe it. It's terrifying, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel emotionally upset after one of these eating episodes is over?\n\nPatient: Oh, God, yes. Upset doesn't even begin to cover it, honestly. It's... it's this incredibly intense wave of just pure self-hatred and guilt that hits me the second it's over. Like, the minute the food is gone and I kind of snap back into reality, it's like a physical weight dropping on my chest. \n\nI'll be sitting there, looking at all the empty wrappers and boxes, and the reality of what I just did comes crashing down. That's what goes through my head. \"You're pathetic, you're weak, you're disgusting.\" \n\nAnd then the panic sets in about the consequences. I usually end up bagging all the trash up immediately—like, double-bagging it so no one can see what's inside—and sneaking it out to the dumpster in the middle of the night because I'm so terrified my roommates or neighbors will see the sheer volume of garbage and know what a freak I am. \n\nSo, yeah, emotionally upset is... it's a huge understatement. It ruins the rest of my night, and usually, the guilt carries over into the next day, too, making it even harder to concentrate at work, which just feeds right back into the stress I was telling you about. Because, like, when I'm at work, I'm usually just inhaling a salad at my desk while I'm staring at three different monitors trying to reconcile accounts, so I guess I eat pretty fast then, just out of necessity. Like, almost every single week since they announced the big restructuring at my firm. They laid off a bunch of people, and suddenly I was managing accounts that used to be handled by three different managers, and the pressure just skyrocketed. And looking at the dates, that's exactly when this all really kicked into high gear. It's not just a once-in-a-while thing, either, like, \"Oh, I had a bad day and ate too much.\" It's usually happening more than once a week. Sometimes it's Tuesday and Thursday, sometimes it's Wednesday, Friday, and Sunday. It's definitely not just here and there. It's becoming this regular, horrible routine that I can't seem to break, no matter how much I try to budget my money or schedule my time. It's just... it's happening a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel stressed, anxious, or upset right before these eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Stressed? Oh, um... well, it's hard to say exactly. I mean, my whole life is basically one giant ball of stress right now, so it's kind of difficult to pinpoint if I'm *specifically* feeling that way right before I start eating, you know? Like, I was just saying about the restructuring at the firm—ever since that happened, I feel like I've been operating at a ten out of ten on the anxiety scale basically from the moment I wake up until I finally manage to pass out at night. \n\nI'm constantly worried about messing up one of these new accounts, or missing a deadline, or that my boss is going to realize I'm in way over my head and fire me. So, by the time I get home, I'm already so wound up. But right before I actually start eating... I don't know if I'd say I feel a sudden spike in being upset or anxious right in that exact moment. It's more like a constant, low-level hum of panic that's just always there in the background now. \n\nSometimes I'll be sitting at my desk at home, trying to get ahead on some spreadsheets for the next day, and I just feel so completely drained and overwhelmed by it all. And then the next thing I know, I'm ordering the food. So, I guess I'm always stressed, but I haven't really noticed if there's a specific trigger or a sudden wave of emotion right before the eating starts. It all just kind of blends together into this one big, exhausting blur of pressure and then... eating. Does that make sense? I just haven't really paid attention to that exact moment before it happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop yourself from continuing to eat once you've started?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean, that's exactly it. It's like I was saying earlier, it's this total inability to just... stop. It's the craziest thing because, like I mentioned, I'm usually so disciplined. I mean, you don't get to be a financial manager at nineteen without having a ridiculous amount of self-control and focus, right? I skipped so many parties in college just to study and get ahead, so I know I have willpower. But with this... it's like my willpower just evaporates the second I take that first bite. \n\nOnce I start, it honestly feels like I'm completely disconnected from my own body. Like I'm just hovering there, watching myself keep reaching for the next thing, and the next thing. And my brain is fully active! I'm sitting there, literally thinking, *Jaime, stop, what are you doing? You have that huge budget report to finish for the partners tomorrow, you need to stop right now.* But my hand just keeps moving. I'll be eating something, and I'll realize I'm not even enjoying it anymore. Like, my stomach will be hurting, the food doesn't even taste good, and I'll be thinking, *Why am I still eating this stale cookie?* But I just can't make myself stop. It's like somebody else is driving the car, and I'm just trapped in the passenger seat watching it all happen until there's literally nothing left in front of me. So yeah, unable to stop is exactly how I'd describe it. It's terrifying, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel emotionally upset after one of these eating episodes is over?\n\nPatient: Oh, God, yes. Upset doesn't even begin to cover it, honestly. It's... it's this incredibly intense wave of just pure self-hatred and guilt that hits me the second it's over. Like, the minute the food is gone and I kind of snap back into reality, it's like a physical weight dropping on my chest. \n\nI'll be sitting there, looking at all the empty wrappers and boxes, and the reality of what I just did comes crashing down. And I just start crying, sometimes for a really long time. I feel so disgusted with myself. I mean, I'm sitting there, nineteen years old, supposed to be this put-together professional managing millions of dollars for other people, and I can't even manage my own eating? It's pathetic. That's what goes through my head. \"You're pathetic, you're weak, you're disgusting.\" \n\nAnd then the panic sets in about the consequences. I start calculating the calories in my head, just like I calculate financial projections at work, and the numbers are always so horrifying. I feel so bloated and sick, physically, but the emotional part is worse. I'll just curl up on my bed, feeling so ashamed that I can't even look at myself in the mirror. I usually end up bagging all the trash up immediately—like, double-bagging it so no one can see what's inside—and sneaking it out to the dumpster in the middle of the night because I'm so terrified my roommates or neighbors will see the sheer volume of garbage and know what a freak I am. \n\nSo, yeah, emotionally upset is... it's a huge understatement. It ruins the rest of my night, and usually, the guilt carries over into the next day, too, making it even harder to concentrate at work, which just feeds right back into the stress I was telling you about. It's just a miserable, miserable feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating much more quickly than usual during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Faster? Um, I don't... I don't really know if I'd say I'm eating *faster* than I normally do. I mean, I'm not really sure what my \"normal\" speed even is anymore, to be perfectly honest. Because, like, when I'm at work, I'm usually just inhaling a salad at my desk while I'm staring at three different monitors trying to reconcile accounts, so I guess I eat pretty fast then, just out of necessity. You know, time is money and all that, especially in finance. \n\nBut during these... these episodes at home... I haven't really noticed if my pace is actually quicker. It's more about the sheer volume of it all, and that feeling of being completely out of control that I was talking about before. Like, I might be eating fast, or I might just be eating steadily for a really, really long time. I honestly lose track of time when it's happening. It could be twenty minutes, it could be an hour, I just don't know. \n\nI'm so zoned out, just focused on getting the next bite into my mouth, that I'm not really paying attention to how fast my hands are moving or how quickly I'm chewing. It's just this continuous, robotic motion. So, I guess I can't really say for sure if the speed itself is faster than how I usually eat. It's more the... the intensity of it, I suppose? But the actual speed... yeah, I really haven't paid attention to that specific detail. I'm usually too caught up in the panic of it all to notice if I'm chewing faster than normal.\n\nDoctor: Do you regularly have episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. That's actually the main reason I finally made the appointment to come in here. It’s almost always right after I get home from work, or sometimes after my evening classes. I’ll walk in the door, completely exhausted, you know, just drained from staring at spreadsheets or listening to lectures all day. I’ll tell myself I’m just going to relax, so I sit down on my couch with my laptop, maybe put on a show or just try to unwind for a minute. \n\nAnd then... it just starts. I’ll have ordered a whole takeout meal on the way home, telling myself I’ll have half for dinner and save the rest for lunch the next day. But I end up eating the entire thing. And then, once that’s gone, it’s like a switch flips, and I’m just looking for more. I’ll get into the family-size bag of chips, and then I’ll move on to the sweets. I have this desk drawer where I keep snacks, mostly stuff I brought back from the office pantry, and I’ll just start emptying it out. Chocolate, granola bars, whatever is in there. \n\nIt’s just... it’s an unbelievable amount of food. Way, way more than I ever intended to eat when I sat down. And it happens in this really condensed window of time, usually before I even get up to change out of my work clothes. It’s so much more than I would ever eat in front of my roommates or my coworkers. If we go out for a team lunch, I’m the girl picking at a salad, you know? But then I get home, and I just consume this massive, massive quantity of food all by myself, hidden away in my room. It’s like two different lives, and the amount I eat when I’m alone with my laptop is just... it’s staggering, honestly. It’s why I knew I needed to talk to someone, because the sheer volume is just completely out of control.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you are in control of what you are eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. That's the scariest part, honestly. It's like... it's like someone else is driving the car, and I'm just trapped in the passenger seat watching it happen. It's the most bizarre, terrifying feeling. I mean, I'm a financial manager, right? My entire day is about control. I control portfolios, I control risk, I control spreadsheets down to the last decimal point. If a client calls and wants to move funds, I execute it perfectly. I'm in charge. But then I get home, and this... this thing happens, and all that control just vanishes completely.\n\nIt's like my brain splits in two. Once I start eating, it feels like I'm watching myself keep reaching for the next thing, and the next thing, and the rational part of my brain is screaming at me. I'm literally sitting there thinking, *Jaime, stop, you have a budget report to finish for tomorrow morning, you have a midterm to study for, and you're not even enjoying this anymore.* I know I'm not hungry. I know it's going to make me feel sick. I know I'm going to hate myself afterward. But my hand just keeps moving. It's like I'm on autopilot, just ripping open another wrapper, taking another bite, swallowing without even tasting it. \n\nI'll tell myself, \"Okay, just one more cookie, and then I'm closing the box.\" And I'll say it out loud, even! But then the cookie is gone, and my hand is already back in the box before I've even processed it. I can't stop. I literally cannot make myself stop until I'm physically in pain or the food is completely gone. It's so humiliating, because I'm usually such a disciplined person. I juggle work and college, I pay my own rent, I manage millions of dollars... but I can't stop myself from eating a sleeve of Oreos when I know I don't even want them. So, no, there is zero control. It's like my body just takes over and my mind is just a helpless witness to the whole disaster.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or experience significant distress after these eating episodes are over?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Oh... yeah. Yeah, absolutely. I mean, the distress is... it's overwhelming. It really is. It’s not just a fleeting \"Oh, I shouldn't have eaten that\" kind of feeling. It’s this deep, sinking, horrible weight that settles right in my chest the second it's over. And the transition is so abrupt, you know? One minute I'm in that weird, zoned-out trance I was telling you about, just eating and eating, and then suddenly the food is gone, or I'm in so much physical pain that I literally can't swallow another bite, and... the fog just lifts. And the reality of what I just did crashes down on me so hard.\n\nI look around my room, and I just see the wrappers, the empty takeout containers, the crumbs everywhere... and the guilt is just suffocating. I'll sit there on my bed, staring at my laptop screen, which usually has some spreadsheet or lecture notes pulled up that I completely ignored, and I just start crying. I cry because I feel so weak, so entirely defeated by my own body. I think about how hard I work all day, trying to be this professional, put-together financial manager, trying to keep my grades up in my classes, trying to show everyone that I have it all figured out at nineteen... and then I look at the pile of trash on my floor and I just feel like a total fraud. \n\nAnd the physical discomfort just amplifies the emotional distress, right? Because my stomach will be stretched so tight it hurts to breathe, and I'll feel nauseous and sweaty, and every physical sensation is just a reminder of my failure. I'll spend the rest of the night just agonizing over it. I'll calculate the calories in my head, which is torture because I'm a numbers person, so I literally can't stop my brain from tallying it all up. And then I start bargaining with myself, like, \"Okay, I'll fast tomorrow,\" or \"I'll spend an extra hour at the gym,\" just trying to figure out how to undo this massive mistake. But you can't undo it. \n\nAnd the shame... I can't even tell you how isolating it is. I live with roommates, and I'm terrified they'll find out. I'll wait until I'm absolutely sure they're asleep, and then I'll sneak out to the kitchen with all my trash shoved into a plastic bag, trying to hide the evidence deep in the bottom of the main garbage can so they won't see it. It's so sneaky and deceptive, and I hate being that kind of person. I pride myself on being honest and straightforward in my job, but here I am, sneaking around my own apartment like a criminal just to hide empty food wrappers. So, yes, the guilt and the distress... it's probably the worst part of the whole thing. It just completely consumes me and ruins the rest of my night, and usually the next day, too.\n\nDoctor: Do you enjoy the taste of the food while you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Enjoy the taste? Honestly... no. Not really. I mean, maybe the very first bite? Like, if I ordered something specific, that first bite might register, but after that... it's just... nothing. It's so weird to explain, because if you look at the sheer amount of junk food I'm eating, you'd probably think, \"Oh, she's indulging, she's just having a great time eating all her favorite treats.\" But it's not like that at all. \n\nIt's not like going to a nice restaurant where you're savoring a meal and paying attention to the spices or whatever. It's completely mechanical. I'm just chewing and swallowing as fast as I can. Sometimes I'll get to the bottom of a family-size bag of something and realize I didn't even register a single flavor. It could literally be cardboard, honestly. It's more about just the physical act of eating, like the repetitive motion of my jaw moving and my hand reaching for the next thing. It's almost like I'm trying to numb out my brain, just drowning out all the spreadsheets and the client calls and the study guides with this mindless, physical task. \n\nAnd that actually makes the guilt so much worse, you know? Because if I'm going to consume all that food and completely ruin my whole night and make myself physically ill, shouldn't I at least get some pleasure out of it? Shouldn't it at least taste amazing? But it doesn't. I remember just last week, I bought this expensive, fancy chocolate bar from the cafe near my office building. I told myself I was just going to have one square as a reward for finishing a massive quarterly report. But then I got home, the episode started, and I ate the entire thing in probably thirty seconds. Afterward, I couldn't even tell you if it was dark chocolate or milk chocolate, or if it had almonds in it. I literally didn't taste it. It just disappeared into the void along with everything else. \n\nIt's like my tastebuds just completely shut off the second my brain goes into that weird autopilot mode I was telling you about. So no, enjoyment isn't really part of the equation at all. It's just a compulsion, just this frantic need to consume until I physically can't anymore, regardless of what it actually tastes like.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you are unable to control how much you eat during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly like I was saying earlier, it's just a total loss of control over the whole thing, especially the amount. It's like I mentioned before—once I start, it really feels like I’m just watching myself keep reaching for the next thing, and the next thing. And the whole time, the logical part of my brain is right there, and I'm literally thinking to myself, Jaime, stop, you have a budget report to finish and you’re not even enjoying this anymore. \n\nBut it doesn't matter how loud that voice is. The volume of food just keeps escalating, and I can't rein it in. I'll look at what I've gone through, and it's staggering. As a financial manager, my entire career is based on quantifying things, measuring things, knowing exactly how much of an asset is going where. I track everything. I manage my college courses the same way, very strictly scheduled. But during these episodes, I can't portion anything. I can't just have a normal amount. I'll start with a box of crackers, and I'll think I'm just having a handful, but then my hand just keeps going back into the box over and over again. \n\nI'm sitting there, fully aware of all the responsibilities I have waiting for me, and I know I should stop, but I just can't regulate how much I'm taking in. I can't limit it to a serving size. It doesn't stop until there is physically nothing left to eat or I'm in too much pain to swallow. So the quantity is completely out of my hands, just like everything else. I just can't stop it once it starts.\n\nDoctor: How often do you experience these episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short time?\n\nPatient: How often? Well... it's funny you ask that, because, well, not funny, but you know how I said I'm really meticulous about tracking things? My budget, my schedule, all the numbers? \n\nI actually sat down the other night—because the guilt was just completely eating at me after an episode—and I forced myself to look back through my personal journal and my bank statements. Specifically, I was looking at all my food delivery charges. DoorDash, UberEats, all of those. And... it was honestly terrifying to see it all laid out in black and white like that. Because when you're in the middle of it, you try to convince yourself it's just a bad day, right? You tell yourself, \"Oh, I was just really stressed this week, it's a one-off.\" \n\nBut looking at the actual data... these episodes have been happening most weeks. Usually more than once a week, actually. It's definitely not just here and there anymore. It's a very clear, undeniable pattern. \n\nAnd when I really traced the timeline back, I realized it all started picking up right around the time the restructuring started at my firm. I don't think I mentioned that to you yet, but my company is going through this massive departmental restructuring right now. And as a financial manager, especially being nineteen and constantly feeling like I have to prove I deserve to be in that room, the pressure has just been astronomical. I've been pulling these insane hours, trying to manage the transition while also trying to balance my college coursework, and... I don't know, I guess the stress of the restructuring just broke something in my routine. \n\nBut yeah, looking at those delivery charges... it's just so much money, too. Which just absolutely hurts my soul as a finance person, you know? I'll look at my statement and see a charge for forty dollars at a bakery, and then another thirty dollars at a fast-food place literally an hour later, all on a random Tuesday night. And then I'll see the exact same pattern on Thursday. And maybe again on Sunday. So, yes, to answer your question, it's happening most weeks, usually multiple times a week, ever since that restructuring began. It's just... it's a lot to process when you see the actual frequency written down on paper like that. You can't hide from it anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating this way helps you cope with the stress and anxiety you feel from work and school?\n\nPatient: Help me cope? I... I don't know. I mean, I really don't know if \"help\" is the right word for it at all. Because, like, on one hand, you'd think it must be doing *something* for me, right? Otherwise, why would I keep doing it? I'm a very logical person. I deal with spreadsheets and risk assessments and cost-benefit analyses all day long. If an investment isn't yielding a return, you cut it, you know? So, logically, my brain wants to say there must be some kind of return here, some kind of stress relief or coping mechanism happening...\n\nBut when I actually sit down and think about it... I honestly can't say if it helps. I'm really not sure it does. Because the anxiety from work—like just yesterday, for example, I had this massive portfolio review with the senior partners, and I'm sitting there at the head of the conference table, nineteen years old, trying to project total confidence while inside I'm just completely spiraling about whether my projection models are correct. And on top of that, I'm sitting there wondering if my coursework for my macroeconomics class is going to get done before the midnight deadline... \n\nAnd then I go home, the pressure is just boiling over, and I have one of these eating episodes. But during it, like I said before, I'm just completely numb. I'm not processing anything. And afterward? Afterward, the original anxiety from work and school is still sitting right there waiting for me, except now it's buried under this huge, overwhelming layer of guilt and physical sickness. \n\nSo I haven't really noticed it actually *helping* the stress at all. If anything, it just hits the pause button on my brain for maybe twenty minutes, and then makes everything ten times worse because now I'm stressed about the firm's restructuring *and* the fact that I just spent another fifty dollars on junk food and ruined my stomach for the entire night. So... yeah, I don't know. I really don't have a good answer for that. It's just a thing that happens when the pressure builds up, but I can't say I've noticed it actually relieving any of that pressure in any real, measurable way. I've tried to analyze it, I really have, but the math just doesn't add up."
